      minhash: NiO0fzOBJeUjjuahUOjyFQHIhcV1KrNSlPxA8GhW0BOeez/jORzOvqPjGmigSr9bXihcOaBAYqHHG2u9FTjDpV3RWO8quMK3NlyvmW2ih3wxt8kwtYKFMvmublbpYnvNuN6rmu0BF1qa+/5U4TgFPI/SEkNIBwECISJ53o8ZRw09rk/EjyHmXlzCdhrxQrKDt+ceh/evzIkKEbFkMSqW/pL7o48dakuTumcBCc+Aibcx7ErHFcSryX4F/uQ/KOsMmQYT57PU4pa/8DpbMhi95664RiZUzYnZP/2tJglteTdER1ovF7q8KvqhjJpDElYa2yS3Da+rXj0L2YaUi9TEfg==

      extractor: ax/v0.5.32
---
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:ssm_stability}
For $\gamma, \delta > 0$, define $\mathcal S_{\gamma, \delta}$ as the set of parameter tuples $W$ satisfying $A_n \leq -\gamma$ for every $n \in \{1,\dots,N\}$, where $A_n$ is the $n$-th element of the transition parameter $A$, and $\left[\operatorname{Softplus}(W_\Delta x_t)\right]_d \geq \delta$ for every feature coordinate $d \in \{1,\dots,D\}$ and admissible input token. Consequently, every entry of $\bar A_t$ satisfies: $\left(\bar{A}_t\right)_{n,d} \leq \rho := \exp{(-\gamma\delta)} < 1$.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- assumption-1: body_md -->
\label{assum:lipschitz_cont_loss}
The token loss $\ell_t(\cdot, y_t^*)$ is twice continuously differentiable with respect to its prediction argument. For the parameter tuple $W$ under consideration, there exist finite constants $L_\ell,H_\ell>0$ such that, for every admissible $\xi$, the prediction $y=y_t(W;x)$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
    \textstyle \|\nabla_y\ell_t(y, y_t^*)\|_2\leq L_\ell, \qquad  \|\nabla_y^2\ell_t(y,y_t^*)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq H_\ell.
\end{equation}
<!-- end assumption-1 -->
<!-- assumption-2: body_md -->
\label{assum:bounded_inputs}
There exists $L_x > 0$ such that $\forall t$, $\|x_t\|_2 \leq L_x$ $\forall$ admissible $\xi$.
<!-- end assumption-2 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:ssm_grad_bound} Suppose Assumptions~\ref{assum:lipschitz_cont_loss} and \ref{assum:bounded_inputs} hold for a parameter tuple $W \in \mathcal S_{\gamma, \delta}$. For a single-layer SSM defined in Section~\ref{sec:system_model}, the following bound holds for every finite integer $S \geq 1$ and every admissible length-$S$ sequence pair $\xi \in \mathcal Z$:
\begin{equation}
    \textstyle \left\|\nabla_W \mathcal L^S(W;\xi)\right\|_F \leq \mathcal G_{\text{SSM}}(W) := L_\ell L_x\left(K_B K_\Delta \frac{\sqrt{ND}}{1-\rho} + \|W_C\|_2 \frac{K_W}{1-\rho}\right)
\end{equation}
with
\begin{align}
    \textstyle K_\Delta & \textstyle := \left\|W_\Delta\right\|_2L_x + \sqrt{D}\log(2), ~~~~
    K_B := \left\|W_B\right\|_2 L_x^2, \\
    \textstyle K_W & \textstyle := ND K_BK_{\Delta} \left(K_\Delta + \sqrt{D} \left\|A\right\|_2L_x\right)
    \left(\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}\right) + L_x^2 \sqrt{N} K_\Delta + L_x \sqrt{D} K_B
\end{align}
where $\|\cdot\|_F$ is the Euclidean norm of the block Frobenius norms.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:r_local_neighborhood} For a reference model $\widetilde W$ and radius $R > 0$, define $\mathcal N_R(\widetilde W) := \{W:\|W-\widetilde W\|_F\leq R\}$, where the $\|\cdot\|_F$ is defined for a parameter tuple $W$ as $\|W\|_F:=\left(\|{W_\Delta} \|_F^2+\|{W_B} \|_F^2+\|{W_C} \|_F^2+\|A \|_F^2\right)^{1/2}$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- assumption-3: body_md -->
\label{assum:unbiased_stochastic_gradients}
For every client $k$ and every stable local model neighborhood $\mathcal N_R(W^r)\subseteq\mathcal S_{\gamma,\delta}$, define
\begin{equation}
    g_k(W;\xi) := \nabla_W\mathcal L^S(W;\xi)
\end{equation}
as the stochastic sequence-level gradient for a sampled sequence
$\xi\sim \mathcal P_k$. Then, $\forall~W\in\mathcal N_R(W^r)$
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb E_{\xi\sim \mathcal P_k}\left[g_k(W;\xi)\right] = \nabla_W\mathcal L_k(W)
\end{equation}
holds. At each local update, conditional on all preceding training randomness, the mini-batch consists of $\Xi$ independent sequences drawn from $\mathcal P_k$. The mini-batch gradient is their average. Batches drawn by different clients at the same local step are conditionally independent.
<!-- end assumption-3 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:ssm_local_smoothness}
Let $W^r$ be a fixed reference parameter tuple and let $0<R<\infty$ satisfy $\mathcal N_R(W^r)\subseteq\mathcal S_{\gamma,\delta}$. Suppose Assumptions~\ref{assum:lipschitz_cont_loss}, ~\ref{assum:bounded_inputs}, and ~\ref{assum:unbiased_stochastic_gradients} hold, with finite and differentiable expected local objectives $\mathcal L_k$ on this neighborhood. Furthermore, let the bounds in Assumption~\ref{assum:lipschitz_cont_loss} hold with the same finite constants $L_\ell$ and $H_\ell$ $\forall$ $W\in\mathcal N_R(W^r)$, every admissible input-target sequence pair $\xi\in\mathcal Z$, and every token position, independently of sequence length.

Then, for every client $k$, $\mathcal L_k$ is $L_{\mathrm{SSM},R}$-smooth on $\mathcal N_R(W^r)$. Specifically, for all
$W,V\in\mathcal N_R(W^r)$,
\begin{equation}
    \|\nabla_W\mathcal L_k(W)
      -\nabla_W\mathcal L_k(V)\|_F
    \leq
    L_{\mathrm{SSM},R}\|W-V\|_F,
\end{equation}
where $L_{\mathrm{SSM},R}$ is defined
in~\eqref{eq:L_ssm_definition}.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:supp_single_fedavg}
Under Assumptions~\ref{assum:lipschitz_cont_loss}-~\ref{assum:unbiased_stochastic_gradients}, the conditions specified in Definitions~\ref{def:ssm_stability}-~\ref{def:r_local_neighborhood}, and the conditions of
Theorem~\ref{thm:fedavg_ssm_convergence}, let $M_B,M_C,M_\Delta,$ and $M_A$ denote uniform upper bounds on $\left\|W_B\right\|_2,\left\|W_C\right\|_2,\left\|W_\Delta\right\|_2,$ and $\left\|A\right\|_2$, respectively, over the $R$-local neighborhoods $\mathcal N_R(W^r)$, and define
\begin{equation*}
    M_R := (1 + M_B)(1 + M_C)(1 + M_\Delta)(1 + M_\Delta + M_A).
\end{equation*}
We further define $\bar{\mathcal P}:=\sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathcal P_k$, $\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}} := \sum_{k=1}^K p_k \operatorname{TV}(\mathcal P_k,\bar{\mathcal P})^2,$ where $\operatorname{TV}$ denotes the total variation distance. Under these conditions, for FedAvg applied to the single-layer selective SSM defined in Section~\ref{sec:system_model}, the global optimization trajectory $\{W^r\}_{r=0}^T$ satisfies
\begin{equation*} 
    \frac{1}{T}\sum_{r=0}^{T-1} \mathbb E \left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 = \mathcal O\left(\frac{f\left(W^0\right)-f\left(W^*\right)}{\eta ET} + \frac{\eta M_R^4}{\Xi(1-\rho)^8} +  \frac{\eta^2 M_R^6(E-1) }{(1-\rho)^{12}}\left(\frac{1}{\Xi}+E\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}\right)\right).
\end{equation*}
The quantities $N,D,L_x,L_\ell,$ and $H_\ell$ affect only the implicit constant and do not alter the displayed scaling with the optimization parameters, parameter envelopes, stability, or sequence length.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- head-1: body_md -->
Standard FedAvg convergence bounds assume smoothness and bounds on stochastic gradient variance and client-gradient disagreement. In contrast, our analysis derives these quantities from the selective SSM recurrence. We first bound the hidden states and their parameter derivatives to obtain the gradient bound. The second-order analysis then establishes local smoothness, while the remaining lemmas translate the gradient bound into bounds on stochastic variance and client disagreement. Together, these results yield a convergence rate whose model dependence is explicit in the parameter envelopes $M_B,M_C,M_\Delta,M_A$ and the stability factor $\rho$, with dimensions and input and loss regularity constants fixed. As a result, the corollary identifies which SSM parameters enter the convergence guarantee without requiring a separately assumed smoothness or gradient-dissimilarity constant for the model.

The derived bound distinguishes sequence length from recurrent memory: the gradient and smoothness estimates contain no explicit sequence-length factor, but increase as the recurrent stability factor $\rho$ approaches one, allowing past state information to persist for longer. This dependence is stronger in the additional local-drift terms, which contain $M_R^6/(1-\rho)^{12}$, as opposed to the baseline stochastic term, which contains $M_R^4/(1-\rho)^8$. Consequently, the bound assigns a larger penalty to performing multiple local updates when $\rho$ is close to one. However, $\rho$ alone does not determine this penalty: even for the same $\rho$ and client distributions, larger parameter envelopes increase the coefficient multiplying $\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}$. The analysis therefore separates the amount of distribution mismatch from the influence of the SSM parameters on its contribution to the convergence bound. This heterogeneity contribution vanishes at $E=1$, but does not decrease with batch size when $E>1$. Our synthetic experiments observe increasing client disagreement as $\rho$ approaches one, supporting this qualitative connection without establishing the worst-case exponents.

Furthermore, the radius $R$ determines how the broadcast model’s weight norms are enlarged to obtain bounds valid throughout local training: each block satisfies $\|W_X\|_2\leq\|W_X^r\|_2+R$. As such, allowing greater client displacement increases the parameter envelopes entering the convergence bound, even when $\rho$ remains unchanged. Additional local steps can therefore affect the guarantee through both the explicit dependence on $E$ and the larger envelopes needed to cover the resulting trajectories. These trajectories must also remain within a neighborhood satisfying $\mathcal N_R(W^r)\subseteq\mathcal S_{\gamma,\delta}$. The FedProx extension in Appendix~\ref{appendix:fed_algs_convergence_bounds_proofs} demonstrates how restricting client movement changes the guarantee: its effective radius $R_\mu\leq R$ permits smaller envelopes $M_X^\mu=M_X-R+R_\mu$, reducing the SSM-dependent factors multiplying noise and heterogeneity. However, the proximal term also increases local smoothness by \(\mu\). The resulting bound therefore exposes a trade-off between tighter control of the SSM parameters and the additional smoothness cost of proximal regularization.
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:local_lipschit_SSM_grad_bound}
Let $W^r$ be some fixed reference parameter tuple and let $0 < R < \infty$ be a finite radius such that $\mathcal N_R(W^r)\subseteq\mathcal S_{\gamma,\delta}$. Suppose Assumptions~\ref{assum:lipschitz_cont_loss} and~\ref{assum:bounded_inputs} hold with the neighborhood-uniform constants specified in Section~\ref{subsec:theoretical_convergence_standard_federated_baselines}.

Let $\mathcal G_{\mathrm{SSM}}(W)$ be the bounding function defined in Theorem~\ref{thm:ssm_grad_bound}. Then $\mathcal G_{\mathrm{SSM}}$ is Lipschitz continuous on $\mathcal N_R(W^r)$. 

Specifically, with $N,D,L_\ell,L_x,\gamma,\delta$ fixed, there
exists a finite constant
$L_{\mathcal G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}}>0$, depending only
on $R$ and the block norms
$\|W_\Delta^r\|_2$, $\|W_B^r\|_2$,
$\|W_C^r\|_2$, and $\|A^r\|_2$, such that
for all $W,V\in\mathcal N_R(W^r)$,
\begin{equation}
    |\mathcal G_{\mathrm{SSM}}(W)
      -\mathcal G_{\mathrm{SSM}}(V)|
    \leq
    L_{\mathcal G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}}\|W-V\|_F.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- head-2: body_md -->
Fix the reference tuple $W^r$ and the radius $R$. Throughout this proof, $N,D,L_\ell,L_x,\gamma,\delta$ are constant as $W$ and $V$ vary over the neighborhood $\mathcal N_R(W^r)$. In particular, $\rho=\exp(-\gamma\delta)$ is the same for both
parameter tuples.

First, by Theorem~\ref{thm:ssm_grad_bound}, $\mathcal G_{\text{SSM}}(W)$ has an explicit definition:
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal G_{\text{SSM}}(W) = L_\ell L_x\left(K_BK_\Delta\frac{\sqrt{ND}}{1-\rho}+\left\|W_C\right\|_2\frac{K_W}{1-\rho}\right),
\end{equation}
where $W$ is the tuple of selective SSM weights $(W_\Delta, W_B,W_C,A)$.

Since $W=(W_\Delta, W_B, W_C, A)$ is a parameter tuple, given another parameter tuple $V = (V_\Delta, V_B, V_C, V_A)$ we use the block Frobenius norm:
\begin{equation}
    \|W-V\|_F^2:=\|W_\Delta-V_\Delta\|_F^2 + \|W_B-V_B\|_F^2+\|W_C-V_C\|_F^2+\|A-V_A\|_F^2.
\end{equation}
For each parameter block $X \in \{\Delta, B, C, A\}$, let $W_X$ denote the corresponding block of $W$, and let $W_X^r$ denote the corresponding block of the central model $W^r$ (for notational uniformity, we write $W_A:=A$ and $W_A^r:=A^r$). Given the central model $W^r$, we evaluate the maximum possible value of a norm of each parameter matrix within the neighborhood $\mathcal N_R(W^r)$. Since $W\in\mathcal N_R(W^r)$, each block satisfies
\begin{equation} 
    \|W_X-W_X^r\|_F \leq \|W-W^r\|_F \leq R.
\end{equation}

Therefore, by the triangle inequality and the fact that $\|\cdot\|_2\leq \|\cdot\|_F$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:block_neighborhood_bound}
    \|W_X\|_2 \leq \|W_X^r\|_2+\|W_X-W_X^r\|_2 \leq \|W_X^r\|_2+\|W_X-W_X^r\|_F \leq \|W_X^r\|_2+R.
\end{equation}

Next, define:
\begin{align}
    M_\Delta &:= \left\|W_\Delta^r\right\|_2+R \label{eq:m_delta} \\ 
    M_B &:= \left\|W_B^r\right\|_2+R \label{eq:m_B_init} \\
    M_C &:= \left\|W_C^r\right\|_2+R \label{eq:m_C} \\
    M_A &:= \left\|A^r\right\|_2+R \label{eq:m_A}
\end{align}
Then, by \eqref{eq:block_neighborhood_bound}, it is true that for all individual matrices $W_B, W_C, W_\Delta, A$, the following holds:
\begin{align}
    \left\|W_\Delta\right\|_2  &\leq M_\Delta \\
    \left\|W_B\right\|_2 &\leq M_B \\
    \left\|W_C\right\|_2  &\leq M_C \\
    \left\|A\right\|_2  &\leq M_A 
\end{align}
The same bounds also hold for the corresponding blocks of any $V\in\mathcal N_R(W^r)$.

We further define:
\begin{align}
    M_{K_\Delta} &:= M_{\Delta} L_x + \sqrt{D}\log(2) \label{eq:m_k_delta} \\
    M_{K_B} &:= M_BL_x^2 \label{eq:m_k_b}
\end{align}

Next, we evaluate the Lipschitz constants for individual components of the expression for $\mathcal G_{\text{SSM}}(W)$. We'll utilize the property that: $\left|\left\|X\right\|_2-\left\|Y\right\|_2\right| \leq \left\|X-Y\right\|_2 \leq \left\|X-Y\right\|_F$.

First:
\begin{align}
    \left|K_\Delta(W)-K_\Delta(V)\right| &\leq L_x\left|\left\|W_\Delta\right\|_2-\left\|V_\Delta\right\|_2\right| \leq L_x\left\|W_\Delta-V_\Delta\right\|_F \leq  L_x \left\|W-V\right\|_F \\
    \left|K_B(W)-K_B(V)\right| &\leq L_x^2\left|\left\|W_B\right\|_2-\left\|V_B\right\|_2\right| \leq L_x^2\left\|W_B-V_B\right\|_F \leq L_x^2 \left\|W-V\right\|_F  
\end{align}
So the Lipschitz constants for the base functions are:
\begin{align}
    L_{K_\Delta} &= L_x \\
    L_{K_B} &= L_x^2 \\
    L_C &= 1 \text{ (for the term } \left\|W_C\right\|_2 \text{)} \\
    L_A &= 1 \text{ (for the term } \left\|A\right\|_2 \text{)}
\end{align}

Next, by the product rule for Lipschitz functions, if $|f| \leq M_f$ and $|g| \leq M_g$ and are Lipschitz with constants $L_f$ and $L_g$ on $\mathcal N_R(W^r)$, then $fg$ is Lipschitz with constant
\begin{equation}
    L_{fg} = M_fL_g + M_gL_f.
\end{equation}

Given that, let
\begin{equation}
    P_1(W) := K_B(W)K_\Delta(W).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
    L_{P_1} = M_{K_B} L_{K_\Delta} + M_{K_\Delta} L_{K_B} = M_{K_B} L_x + M_{K_\Delta} L_x^2,
\end{equation}
and 
\begin{equation}
    M_{P_1} = M_{K_B} M_{K_\Delta}.
\end{equation}

Next, define
\begin{equation}
    P_2(W) := K_\Delta(W) + \sqrt{D}\|A\|_2L_x.
\end{equation}
Then, by the sum rule of Lipschitz functions:
\begin{equation}
    L_{P_2} = L_{K_\Delta} + \sqrt{D} L_A L_x = L_x + \sqrt{D}L_x,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
    M_{P_2} = M_{K_\Delta} + \sqrt{D}M_AL_x.
\end{equation}

Continuing, define
\begin{equation}
    P_3(W) := P_1(W)P_2(W).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
    L_{P_3} = M_{P_1}L_{P_2} + M_{P_2}L_{P_1},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
    M_{P_3} = M_{P_1}M_{P_2}.
\end{equation}

Given these definitions, and using the definition of $K_W$ from Theorem~\ref{thm:ssm_grad_bound}, a valid Lipschitz constant for $K_W$ is:
\begin{equation}
    L_{K_W} = ND\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}L_{P_3} + L_x^3\sqrt{N} + L_x^3\sqrt{D},
\end{equation}
and the upper bound for $K_W(W)$ is:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:M_kw}
    M_{K_W} = ND\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}M_{P_3} + L_x^2\sqrt{N}M_{K_\Delta} + L_x\sqrt{D}M_{K_B}.
\end{equation}

Finally, define
\begin{equation}
    P_4(W) := \|W_C\|_2 K_W(W).
\end{equation}
Since $\|W_C\|_2$ is bounded by $M_C$ and has Lipschitz constant $L_C = 1$, the product rule gives
\begin{equation}
    L_{P_4} = M_C L_{K_W} + M_{K_W}.
\end{equation}

Combining everything together and using linearity, we get the final Lipschitz constant for $\mathcal G_{\text{SSM}}(W)$:
\begin{equation}
    L_{\mathcal G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}} =L_\ell L_x\left(\frac{\sqrt{ND}}{1-\rho}L_{P_1} + \frac{1}{1-\rho}L_{P_4}\right).
\end{equation}

Because $R < \infty$, the block norms of $W^r$ are finite, and $\rho < 1$, all constants $M_{K_\Delta}$, $M_{K_B}$, $M_{P_1}$, $M_{P_2}$, $M_{P_3}$, $M_{K_W}$, $L_{P_1}$, $L_{P_2}$, $L_{P_3}$, $L_{K_W}$, and $L_{P_4}$ are finite. As such, $L_{\mathcal G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}} < \infty$, and $\forall W, V \in \mathcal N_R(W^r)$,
\begin{equation}
    |\mathcal G_{\text{SSM}}(W)-\mathcal G_{\text{SSM}}(V)| \leq L_{\mathcal G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}}\|W-V\|_F.
\end{equation}

This proves that $\mathcal G_{\text{SSM}}(W)$ is locally Lipschitz continuous on $\mathcal N_R(W^r)$. \qed
<!-- end head-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:ssm_fedavg_g_constant} 
Suppose the Assumptions~\ref{assum:lipschitz_cont_loss} and \ref{assum:bounded_inputs} hold. Let $\mathcal N_R(W^r) \subseteq \mathcal S_{\gamma,\delta}$ be a stable local model neighborhood around the round-$r$ broadcast model $W^r$. Let:
\begin{equation}
    g(W;\xi):=\nabla_W\mathcal L^S(W;\xi)
\end{equation}
denote the stochastic sequence-level gradient for a sampled input-target sequence pair $\xi = (x_{1:S}, y_{1:S}^*)$. Then, for every $W \in \mathcal N_R(W^r)$,
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb E_{\xi} \left\|g(W;\xi)\right\|_F^2 \leq G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
    G_{\mathrm{SSM},R} := L_\ell L_x \left( M_{K_B}M_{K_\Delta}\frac{\sqrt{ND}}{1-\rho} + M_C\frac{M_{K_W}}{1-\rho}\right),
\end{equation}
where $M_{K_B}, M_{K_\Delta}, M_C, \text{ and } M_{K_W}$ are defined in~\eqref{eq:m_k_b},~\eqref{eq:m_k_delta},~\eqref{eq:m_C}, and ~\eqref{eq:M_kw} respectively.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- head-3: body_md -->
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ssm_grad_bound}, under the assumptions of the lemma, for every $W \in \mathcal N_R(W^r)$, every finite integer $S \geq 1$, and every admissible length-$S$ sequence pair $\xi \in \mathcal Z$, we have:
\begin{align}
    \left\|\nabla_W \mathcal L^S(W;\xi)\right\|_F \leq L_\ell L_x\left(K_BK_\Delta\frac{\sqrt{ND}}{1-\rho} + \|W_C\|_2\frac{K_W}{1-\rho}\right). \nonumber
\end{align}
Since $g(W;\xi) = \nabla_W\mathcal L^S(W;\xi)$, it follows that:
\begin{equation}
    \|g(W;\xi)\|_F \leq L_\ell L_x\left(K_BK_\Delta\frac{\sqrt{ND}}{1-\rho} + \|W_C\|_2\frac{K_W}{1-\rho}\right).
\end{equation}
Restricting to $W \in \mathcal N_R(W^r)$, by the local neighborhood bounds defined previously (~\eqref{eq:m_k_b},~\eqref{eq:m_k_delta},~\eqref{eq:m_C}, and ~\eqref{eq:M_kw}), 
\begin{align}
     K_B\leq M_{K_B},
    \qquad
    K_\Delta\leq M_{K_\Delta},
    \qquad
    K_W\leq M_{K_W},
    \qquad
    \|W_C\|_2\leq M_C.
\end{align}

Therefore, 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:g_ssm_definition}
    \|g(W;\xi)\|_F \leq L_\ell L_x \left( M_{K_B}M_{K_\Delta}\frac{\sqrt{ND}}{1-\rho} + M_C\frac{M_{K_W}}{1-\rho}\right) = G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}.
\end{equation}
Since both values are non-negative, squaring both sides gives:
\begin{equation}
    \|g(W;\xi)\|_F^2 \leq G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2 = L_\ell^2 L_x^2 \left( M_{K_B}M_{K_\Delta}\frac{\sqrt{ND}}{1-\rho} + M_C\frac{M_{K_W}}{1-\rho}\right)^2
\end{equation}

Finally, taking expectation over the sampled sequence $\xi$ preserves the bound:
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb E_\xi\left[\|g(W;\xi)\|_F^2\right] \leq G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2
\end{equation}
proving the lemma. \qed
<!-- end head-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:ssm_variance_bound}
Suppose the assumptions of Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_fedavg_g_constant} and Assumption~\ref{assum:unbiased_stochastic_gradients} hold. Let
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal L(W):=\mathbb E_{\xi}\left[\mathcal L^S(W;\xi)\right]
\end{equation*}
denote the expected sequence loss over the local data distribution, and let
\begin{equation*}
    g(W;\xi):=\nabla_W\mathcal L^S(W;\xi)
\end{equation*}
denote the single-sequence stochastic gradient. For a mini-batch
$\Xi_\xi=\{\xi_1,\dots,\xi_\Xi\}$ of $\Xi$ independent sampled sequences, define
\begin{equation}
    g_\Xi(W;\Xi_\xi) := \frac{1}{\Xi}\sum_{j=1}^\Xi g(W;\xi_j).
\end{equation}
Then, for every $W\in\mathcal N_R(W^r)$ it follows that
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb E_{\Xi_\xi}\left\|g_\Xi(W;\Xi_\xi)-\nabla_W\mathcal L(W)\right\|_F^2 \leq \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R,\Xi}^2,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
   \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R,\Xi}^2 := \frac{1}{\Xi}\sup_{W\in\mathcal N_R(W^r)}\left(\mathbb E_{\xi}\|g(W;\xi)\|_F^2 - \|\nabla_W\mathcal L(W)\|_F^2\right). 
\end{equation}
Moreover, by Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_fedavg_g_constant},
\begin{equation}
    \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R,\Xi}^2 \leq \frac{G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2}{\Xi}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- head-4: body_md -->
By unbiasedness and independence of the sampled sequences, the standard mini-batch variance identity gives:
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb E_{\Xi_\xi}\left[\left\|g_\Xi(W;\Xi_\xi)-\nabla_W\mathcal L(W)\right\|_F^2\right] = \frac{1}{\Xi}\mathbb E_{\xi}\left[\left\|g(W;\xi)-\nabla_W\mathcal L(W)\right\|_F^2\right].
\end{align*}
Using the identity
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb E_{\xi}\left[\left\|g(W;\xi)-\nabla_W\mathcal L(W)\right\|_F^2\right] = \mathbb E_{\xi}\|g(W;\xi)\|_F^2 - \|\nabla_W\mathcal L(W)\|_F^2,
\end{equation}
and taking the supremum over $W\in\mathcal N_R(W^r)$, we obtain
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb E_{\Xi_\xi}\left[\left\|g_\Xi(W;\Xi_\xi)-\nabla_W\mathcal L(W)\right\|_F^2\right] \leq \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R,\Xi}^2.
\end{equation}

Finally, Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_fedavg_g_constant} gives
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb E_{\xi}\|g(W;\xi)\|_F^2 \leq G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2,
\end{equation*}
and since $\|\nabla_W\mathcal L(W)\|_F^2\geq 0$,
\begin{equation}
    \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R,\Xi}^2 \leq \frac{G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2}{\Xi}.
\end{equation}
This proves the claim. \qed
<!-- end head-4 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:ssm_gamma_tv_bound}
Suppose the assumptions of Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_fedavg_g_constant} and Assumption~\ref{assum:unbiased_stochastic_gradients} hold. Let
\begin{equation}
    f(W) := \sum_{k=1}^K p_k \mathcal L_k(W)
\end{equation}
be the global objective, where $p_k \geq 0$ and $\sum_{k=1}^K p_k=1$. Let $\mathcal P_k$ denote the local data distribution of client $k$, and define the global mixture distribution as:
\begin{equation}
    \bar{\mathcal P} := \sum_{k=1}^K p_k \mathcal P_k.
\end{equation}
Define the local client heterogeneity constant
\begin{equation}
    \Gamma_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2:=\sup_{W \in \mathcal N_R(W^r)}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k \left\|\nabla_W \mathcal L_k(W) - \nabla_W f(W) \right\|_F^2.
\end{equation}
Then:
\begin{equation}
    \Gamma_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2\leq 4G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2 \sum_{k=1}^K p_k \operatorname{TV}(\mathcal{P}_k, \bar{\mathcal P})^2,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
    \operatorname{TV}(P,Q):=\sup_A |P(A)-Q(A)|.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- head-5: body_md -->
Recall that for each client $k$, the local objective is:
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal L_k(W) =  \mathbb E_{\xi \sim \mathcal P_k}\left[\mathcal L^S(W;\xi)\right],
\end{equation*}
and, by Assumption~\ref{assum:unbiased_stochastic_gradients},
\begin{align}
    \nabla_W\mathcal L_k(W) = \mathbb E_{\xi\sim \mathcal P_k}\left[g(W;\xi)\right].
\end{align}
Similarly, since
\begin{equation*}
    f(W) := \sum_{k=1}^K p_k \mathcal L_k(W),
\end{equation*}
we have
\begin{equation}
    \nabla_W f(W) = \sum_{k=1}^Kp_k\nabla_W\mathcal L_k(W) = \sum_{k=1}^Kp_k\mathbb E_{\xi\sim \mathcal P_k}\left[g(W;\xi)\right].
\end{equation}

Next, by the definition of the mixture distribution $\bar{\mathcal P}$, we can write this as:
\begin{equation}
     \nabla_W f(W) = \mathbb E_{\xi \sim \bar{\mathcal P}}\left[g(W;\xi)\right].
\end{equation}
Therefore, it follows that
\begin{equation}
    \nabla_W\mathcal L_k(W)-\nabla_W f(W) = \mathbb E_{\xi\sim \mathcal P_k}[g(W;\xi)] - \mathbb E_{\xi\sim \bar{\mathcal P}}[g(W;\xi)].
\end{equation}

By the pointwise bound~\eqref{eq:g_ssm_definition} established in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_fedavg_g_constant}, for every $W \in \mathcal N_R(W^r)$ and every admissible sequence pair $\xi \in \mathcal Z$,
\begin{equation*}
    \left\|g(W;\xi)\right\|_F \leq G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}.
\end{equation*}
Since $\mathcal P_k(\mathcal Z) = \bar{\mathcal P}(\mathcal Z )= 1$, the total-variation inequality for bounded vector-valued functions gives:
\begin{equation}
    \left\|\mathbb E_{\xi\sim \mathcal P_k}[g(W;\xi)] - \mathbb E_{\xi\sim \bar{\mathcal P}}[g(W;\xi)]\right\|_F \leq 2G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}\operatorname{TV}(\mathcal P_k,\bar{\mathcal P}).
\end{equation}
So:
\begin{equation}
    \left\|\nabla_W\mathcal L_k(W)-\nabla_W f(W)\right\|_F^2 \leq 4G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2\operatorname{TV}(\mathcal P_k,\bar{\mathcal P})^2.
\end{equation}

Multiplying both sides by $p_k$ and summing over all clients gives:
\begin{equation}
    \sum_{k=1}^K p_k \left\|\nabla_W\mathcal L_k(W)-\nabla_W f(W) \right\|_F^2 \leq 4G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2\sum_{k=1}^K p_k \operatorname{TV}(\mathcal P_k,\bar{\mathcal P})^2.
\end{equation} 

Since the right-hand side of this inequality is independent of $W$, taking the supremum over $W \in \mathcal N_R(W^r)$ we get:
\begin{equation}
    \Gamma_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2 := \sup_{W \in \mathcal N_{R}(W^r)} \sum_{k=1}^K p_k\left\|\nabla_W \mathcal L_k(W)-\nabla_W f(W)\right\|_F^2 \leq 4G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k \operatorname{TV}(\mathcal P_k,\bar{\mathcal P})^2.
\end{equation}

This proves the SSM client heterogeneity bound. \qed
<!-- end head-5 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:fedavg_ssm_convergence}
Consider a distributed learning framework established in Section~\ref{sec:system_model}, with the objective
\begin{equation*}
    f(W) := \sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathcal L_k(W), \qquad p_k\geq0, \qquad \sum_{k=1}^K p_k=1,
\end{equation*}
where $W$ parameterizes a single-layer selective SSM defined in the same section and each $\mathcal L_k$ is finite and differentiable on the considered neighborhoods.

Let $W^0$ be deterministic with $f(W^0) < \infty$ and suppose $f$ is bounded below by a finite constant $f^*$. 

Consider full-participation FedAvg distributed training algorithm applied to $f(W)$ for $T \geq 1$ communication rounds, $E \geq 1$ local steps per round, $\Xi \geq 1$ mini-batch size, and constant deterministic local step size $\eta > 0$, with the global model evolution described as:
\begin{align*}
    W_k^{r,0} &= W^r, \\ W_k^{r,e+1} &= W_k^{r,e}-\eta g_{k,\Xi}^{r,e}~~ \text{ for }~~ e \in \{0, \dots, E-1\}, \\ W^{r+1} &= \sum_{k=1}^K p_k W_k^{r,E}
\end{align*}
where $g_{k,\Xi}^{r,e}$ is the average of $\Xi$ sequence-level gradients evaluated at $W_k^{r,e}$ with sampling and conditional unbiasedness, and $E, \Xi, T \in \mathbb{Z}_{>0}$.

Suppose this model evolution satisfies, almost surely:
\begin{equation*}
    W_{k}^{r,e} \in \mathcal N_{R}(W^r) \subseteq \mathcal{S}_{\gamma,\delta},~~~~ \left\|W_X^r\right\|_2 + R \leq M_X ~~\forall ~~ X \in \{\Delta,B,C,A\},
\end{equation*}
for every client $k$ and local step $0 \leq e \leq E$, where $W_A:=A$, and $R,\gamma,\delta>0$ and the finite deterministic envelopes $M_X$ are common across rounds. Let Assumptions~\ref{assum:lipschitz_cont_loss}-~\ref{assum:unbiased_stochastic_gradients} hold uniformly on these neighborhoods.

Define $\rho := \exp(-\gamma\delta)$ and auxiliary SSM-specific quantities as follows:
\begin{align*}
    M_{K_\Delta} & := M_\Delta L_x + \sqrt D\log 2, \\
    M_{K_B} & := M_B L_x^2 ,\\
    M_{K_W} & := ND M_{K_B}M_{K_\Delta}\left(M_{K_\Delta} + \sqrt D M_A L_x\right)\frac{\rho}{1-\rho} + L_x^2\sqrt N M_{K_\Delta}   + L_x\sqrt D M_{K_B}, \\ H_R & := \frac{M_{K_B}M_{K_\Delta}\sqrt{ND}}{1-\rho}, \qquad
    J_R := \frac{M_{K_W}}{1-\rho}, \\
    M_{A_{1,R}} & := \rho(M_{K_\Delta} + M_A L_x), \\
    M_{A_{2,R}} & := \rho\left[(M_{K_\Delta} + M_A L_x)^2 + 2L_x + \frac{1}{4}M_A L_x^2\right], \\
    M_{B_{2,R}} & := 2L_x^3 + \frac{1}{4}M_B L_x^4,\\
    M_{H_{2,R}} & := \frac{M_{A_{2,R}}H_R + 2M_{A_{1,R}}J_R + M_{B_{2,R}}}{1-\rho}.
\end{align*}
These quantities determine the common SSM-specific smoothness
and gradient bounds:
\begin{align*}
    L_{\mathrm{SSM},R} & := H_\ell L_x^2(H_R + M_C J_R)^2 + L_\ell L_x(M_C M_{H_{2,R}} + 2J_R), \\
    G_{\mathrm{SSM},R} & := L_\ell L_x(H_R + M_CJ_R). 
\end{align*}
For the client distributions, further define
\begin{equation*}
    \bar{\mathcal P} :=\sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathcal P_k, \qquad \mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}} := \sum_{k=1}^K p_k \operatorname{TV}\left(\mathcal P_k,\bar{\mathcal P}\right)^2,
\end{equation*}
where $\operatorname{TV}(P,Q) := \sup_{\mathcal A} \left|P(\mathcal A)-Q(\mathcal A)\right|$ is the total variation distance, with the supremum taken over measurable sets $\mathcal A$.

Then, for any constant deterministic step size $\eta > 0$ satisfying
\begin{equation*}
    \eta E L_{\mathrm{SSM},R}\leq\frac{1}{2}, \qquad 12\eta^2L_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2E(E-1) \leq 1,
\end{equation*}
the following holds:
\begin{align*}
    \frac{1}{T}\sum_{r=0}^{T-1} \mathbb E\|\nabla f(W^r)\|_F^2 \leq& \frac{4\left(f(W^0)-f^*\right)}{\eta ET}
    + \frac{4\eta L_{\mathrm{SSM},R} G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2}{\Xi} \sum_{k=1}^K p_k^2 \nonumber \\ 
    &+ \frac{6\eta^2 L_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2(E-1)G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2}{\Xi} + 48\eta^2 L_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2 E(E-1) G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}.
\end{align*}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- head-6: body_md -->
We start from a standard nonconvex FedAvg convergence bound applicable to the setting considered here. Previous works establish a general bound (see Supplemental Material~C.6, Equation~(96) of \cite{wang2020tackling}) for smooth local objectives, unbiased stochastic gradients with bounded variance, and bounded client gradient dissimilarity.

For a setting with full client participation, $E$ local SGD steps per client, and objective and aggregation weights $p_k$, the bound in \cite{wang2020tackling} takes the following form:
\begin{equation}
    \frac{1}{T}\sum_{r=0}^{T-1} \mathbb E\|\nabla f(W^r)\|_F^2 \leq \frac{4\left(f(W^0)-f^*\right)}{\eta ET} + 4\eta L\sigma^2\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k^2 + 6\eta^2 L^2 (E-1)\sigma^2 + 12\eta^2L^2E(E-1)\Gamma^2,
    \label{eq:generic_fedavg_bound_appendix}
\end{equation}
whenever $\eta>0$ satisfies
\begin{equation*}
    \eta LE\leq\frac{1}{2}, \qquad 12\eta^2L^2E(E-1) \leq 1.
\end{equation*}
Here, $L$ is a common smoothness constant, $\sigma^2$ bounds the conditional variance of each mini-batch gradient estimator, and $\Gamma^2$ bounds the weighted client gradient disagreement:
\begin{equation}
    \sum_{k=1}^K p_k \|\nabla\mathcal L_k(W)-\nabla f(W)\|_F^2 \leq \Gamma^2.
\end{equation}
Note that the inequality~\eqref{eq:generic_fedavg_bound_appendix} specializes the result in \cite{wang2020tackling} applied to full client participation, $E$ local SGD steps at every client, and aggregation weights equal to the objective weights $p_k$.

We apply this bound to the selective SSM setting by substituting the constants established in our lemmas. First,  Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_local_smoothness}, evaluated with the common envelopes $M_X$, gives
\begin{equation}
    L=L_{\mathrm{SSM},R},
\end{equation}
with $L_{\mathrm{SSM},R}$ defined in Theorem~\ref{thm:fedavg_ssm_convergence}.
Since each neighborhood $\mathcal N_R(W^r)$ is convex and contains all local iterates in round $r$, it also contains their weighted averages and the segment joining $W^r$ to $W^{r+1}$. As such, all smoothness inequalities used in the convergence argument hold with the common constant $L_{\mathrm{SSM},R}$.

Next, Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_fedavg_g_constant} derives the common stochastic gradient second-moment bound $G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2$. Together with the sampling conditions in Assumption~\ref{assum:unbiased_stochastic_gradients}, Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_variance_bound}  establishes the choice
\begin{equation}
    \sigma^2 = \frac{G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2}{\Xi}.
\end{equation}
Finally, Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_gamma_tv_bound} establishes the common heterogeneity bound
\begin{equation}
    \Gamma^2 = 4G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2 \sum_{k=1}^Kp_k \operatorname{TV}(\mathcal P_k,\bar{\mathcal P})^2 = 4G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}^2\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}.
\end{equation}

Substituting these quantities into
\eqref{eq:generic_fedavg_bound_appendix}, we obtain the inequality stated in Theorem~\ref{thm:fedavg_ssm_convergence} whenever $\eta>0$ satisfies the stated conditions with $L=L_{\mathrm{SSM},R}$. \qed
<!-- end head-6 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:fedprox_ssm_convergence}
Consider a distributed learning framework established in Section~\ref{sec:system_model}, with the objective
\begin{equation*}
    f(W) := \sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathcal L_k(W), \qquad p_k\geq0, \qquad \sum_{k=1}^K p_k=1,
\end{equation*}
where $W$ parameterizes a single-layer selective SSM defined in the same section and each $\mathcal L_k$ is finite and differentiable on the considered neighborhoods.

Let $W^0$ be deterministic with $f(W^0) < \infty$ and suppose $f$ is bounded below by a finite constant $f^*$. 

Consider full-participation FedProx distributed training algorithm applied to $f(W)$ for $T \geq 1$ communication rounds, $E \geq 1$ local steps per round, $\Xi \geq 1$ mini-batch size, constant deterministic local step size $\eta > 0$, and constant deterministic proximal coefficient $\mu > 0$, with the global model evolution described as:
\begin{align*}
    W_k^{r,0} &= W^r, \\ W_k^{r,e+1} &= W_k^{r,e}-\eta \left(g_{k,\Xi}^{r,e}+ \mu\left(W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right)\right)~~ \text{ for }~~ e \in \{0, \dots, E-1\}, \\ W^{r+1} &= \sum_{k=1}^K p_k W_k^{r,E}
\end{align*}
where $g_{k,\Xi}^{r,e}$ is the average of $\Xi$ sequence-level gradients evaluated at $W_k^{r,e}$ with sampling and conditional unbiasedness, and $E, \Xi, T \in \mathbb{Z}_{>0}$.

Suppose that, almost surely for every round $r < T$:
\begin{equation}
    W_{k}^{r,e} \in \mathcal N_{R}(W^r) \subseteq \mathcal{S}_{\gamma,\delta},~~~~ \left\|W_X^r\right\|_2 + R \leq M_X ~~\forall ~~ X \in \{\Delta,B,C,A\},
\end{equation}
for every client $k$ and local step $0 \leq e \leq E$, where $W_A:=A$, and $R,\gamma,\delta>0$ and the finite deterministic envelopes $M_X$ are common across rounds. Let Assumptions~\ref{assum:lipschitz_cont_loss}-~\ref{assum:unbiased_stochastic_gradients} hold uniformly on these neighborhoods.

Define $\rho := \exp(-\gamma\delta)$. Assume $0<\eta\mu\leq 1$, and define the effective radius $R_\mu$ by
\begin{equation*}
    R_\mu := \min\left\{R,\eta G_{\mathrm{SSM},R} \sum_{j=0}^{E-1}(1-\eta\mu)^j\right\} = \min\left\{R,\frac{G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}}{\mu}\left[1-(1-\eta\mu)^E\right]\right\},
\end{equation*}
where $G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}$ is defined in Theorem~\ref{thm:fedavg_ssm_convergence}, with the corresponding FedProx parameter envelopes defined as:
\begin{equation*}
    M_X^\mu := M_X - R + R_\mu  ~~\forall ~~ X \in \{\Delta,B,C,A\}.
\end{equation*}

The corresponding auxiliary SSM-specific quantities are then defined as:
\begin{align*}
    M_{K_\Delta}^\mu & := M_\Delta^\mu L_x + \sqrt D\log 2, \\
    M_{K_B}^\mu & := M_B^\mu L_x^2 ,\\
    M_{K_W}^\mu & := ND M_{K_B}^\mu M_{K_\Delta}^\mu\left(M_{K_\Delta}^\mu + \sqrt D M_A^\mu L_x\right)\frac{\rho}{1-\rho} + L_x^2\sqrt N M_{K_\Delta}^\mu   + L_x\sqrt D M_{K_B}^\mu, \\ 
    H_{R_\mu} & := \frac{M_{K_B}^\mu M_{K_\Delta}^\mu \sqrt{ND}}{1-\rho}, \qquad
    J_{R_\mu} := \frac{M_{K_W}^\mu}{1-\rho}, \\
    M_{A_{1,R}}^\mu & := \rho(M_{K_\Delta}^\mu + M_A^\mu L_x), \\
    M_{A_{2,R}}^\mu & := \rho\left[(M_{K_\Delta}^\mu + M_A^\mu L_x)^2 + 2L_x + \frac{1}{4}M_A^\mu L_x^2\right], \\
    M_{B_{2,R}}^\mu & := 2L_x^3 + \frac{1}{4}M_B^\mu L_x^4,\\
    M_{H_{2,R}}^\mu & := \frac{M_{A_{2,R}}^\mu H_{R_\mu} + 2M_{A_{1,R}}^\mu J_{R_\mu} + M_{B_{2,R}}^\mu}{1-\rho}.
\end{align*}

Let:
\begin{align*}
    L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu} & := H_\ell L_x^2(H_{R_\mu} + M_C^\mu J_{R_\mu} )^2 + L_\ell L_x(M_C^\mu M^\mu_{H_{2,R}} + 2J_{R_\mu} ), \\
    L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu} & := L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}+\mu. \\
    G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu} & := L_\ell L_x(H_{R_\mu}  + M_C^\mu J_{R_\mu} ), \\
\end{align*}
denote common deterministic SSM-specific smoothness
and gradient bounds constants evaluated on $\mathcal N_{R_\mu}(W^r)$. 

For the client distributions, further define
\begin{equation*}
    \bar{\mathcal P} :=\sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathcal P_k, \qquad \mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}} := \sum_{k=1}^K p_k \operatorname{TV}\left(\mathcal P_k,\bar{\mathcal P}\right)^2,
\end{equation*}
where $\operatorname{TV}(P,Q) := \sup_{\mathcal A} \left|P(\mathcal A)-Q(\mathcal A)\right|$ is the total variation distance, with the supremum taken over measurable sets $\mathcal A$.

Then, for any constant deterministic step size $\eta > 0$ satisfying
\begin{equation*}
    \eta E L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu} \leq \frac{1}{2}, \qquad 12\eta^2L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2E(E-1) \leq 1, 
\end{equation*}
the following bound holds:
\begin{align*}
    \frac{1}{T}\sum_{r=0}^{T-1} \mathbb E\left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 \leq& \frac{3\left(f(W^0)-f^*\right)}{\eta ET} + \frac{3\eta L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu} G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2}{\Xi} \sum_{k=1}^K p_k^2 \\ 
    &+ \frac{3\eta^2L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2 (E-1)G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2}{\Xi} + 24\eta^2L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2E(E-1)G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}.
\end{align*}
Moreover, almost surely, for every round $r < T$, client $k$,
and local step $0\leq e\leq E$,
\begin{equation*}
    \left\|W_k^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F \leq \min\left\{R,\frac{G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}}{\mu}\left[1-(1-\eta\mu)^e\right]\right\} \leq R_\mu.
\end{equation*}
Consequently, all local iterates and the aggregated model $W^{r+1}$ lie in $\mathcal N_{R_\mu}(W^r)\subseteq\mathcal S_{\gamma,\delta}$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- head-7: body_md -->
The FedProx framework modifies the local update as:
\begin{equation}
    W_{k}^{r,e+1} = W_{k}^{r,e} - \eta \left(g_{k,\Xi}^{r,e} + \mu(W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r)\right), \qquad W_k^{r,0}=W^r.
\end{equation}

Subtracting the broadcast model $W^r$ gives
\begin{equation}
    W_{k}^{r,e+1}-W^r = (1-\eta\mu)(W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r) - \eta g_{k,\Xi}^{r,e}
\end{equation}

Taking Frobenius norms, using $0<\eta\mu\leq 1$, and by the triangle inequality,
\begin{equation}
    \left\|W_{k}^{r,e+1}-W^r\right\|_F \leq (1-\eta\mu)\left\|W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F + \eta\left\|g_{k,\Xi}^{r,e}\right\|_F.
\end{equation}
By the bound~\eqref{eq:g_ssm_definition} established in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_fedavg_g_constant}, every admissible sequence gradient satisfies $\left\|g_k(W;\xi)\right\|_F\leq G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}$ for all $W\in \mathcal{N}_R(W^r)$. As such, the mini-batch gradients satisfy:
\begin{align}
    \left\|g_{k,\Xi}^{r,e}\right\|_F = \left\|\frac{1}{\Xi}\sum_{i=1}^{\Xi}g_k(W^{r,e}_{k};\xi^{r,e}_{k,i})\right\|_F \leq\frac{1}{\Xi}\sum_{i=1}^{\Xi} \left\|g_k(W^{r,e}_{k};\xi^{r,e}_{k,i})\right\|_F \leq G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}
\end{align}
Therefore:
\begin{equation}
    \left\|W_{k}^{r,e+1}-W^r\right\|_F \leq (1-\eta\mu)\left\|W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F + \eta G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}.
\end{equation}
Unrolling the recursion from $W_k^{r,0}=W^r$ for every $0\leq e\leq E$, we get:
\begin{equation}
    \left\|W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F \leq \eta G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}\sum_{e'=0}^{e-1}(1-\eta\mu)^{e'} = \frac{G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}}{\mu}\left[1-(1-\eta\mu)^e\right] 
\end{equation}
Combining this with the established neighborhood bounds $\left\|W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F\leq R$, we get:
\begin{equation}
    \left\|W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F \leq \min{\left(R,\frac{G_{\mathrm{SSM},R}}{\mu}\left[1-(1-\eta\mu)^e\right] \right)}\leq R_\mu,
\end{equation}
and since $p_k\geq 0$ and $\sum_k p_k =1$, it follows that:
\begin{equation}
    \left\|W^{r+1}-W^r\right\|_F =  \left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K}p_k\left(W_{k}^{r,E}-W^r\right)\right\|_F \leq  \sum_{k=1}^{K}p_k\left\|W_{k}^{r,E}-W^r\right\|_F \leq R_\mu.
\end{equation}
This shows that all local iterates as well as $W^{r+1}$ belong to $\mathcal N_{R_\mu}(W^r) \subseteq \mathcal S_{\gamma, \delta}$. Since this neighborhood is convex, the segments used below in the smoothness arguments also remain inside it.

Next, for every $W \in \mathcal N_{R_\mu}(W^r)$ and parameter block $X\in\{\Delta,B,C,A\}$, 
\begin{equation}
    \left\|W_X\right\|_2 \leq \|W_X^r\|_2 + R_\mu \leq M_X - R + R_\mu =: M_X^\mu.
\end{equation}
These $M_X^\mu$ are uniform across rounds and deterministic. Let $L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}$ and $G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}$ denote the SSM smoothness and stochastic-gradient constants constructed using $M_X^\mu$, with the stability, input, and loss-regularity constants remaining unchanged. In particular:
\begin{align}
    M_{K_\Delta}^\mu & := M_\Delta^\mu L_x + \sqrt D\log 2, \\
    M_{K_B}^\mu & := M_B^\mu L_x^2 ,\\
    M_{K_W}^\mu & := ND M_{K_B}^\mu M_{K_\Delta}^\mu \left(M_{K_\Delta}^\mu + \sqrt D M_A^\mu L_x\right)\frac{\rho}{1-\rho} + L_x^2\sqrt N M_{K_\Delta}^\mu + L_x\sqrt D M_{K_B}^\mu, \\ 
    H_{R_\mu} & := \frac{M_{K_B}^\mu M_{K_\Delta}^\mu \sqrt{ND}}{1-\rho}, \qquad J_{R_\mu} := \frac{M_{K_W}^\mu}{1-\rho}, \\
    M_{A_{1,R}}^\mu & := \rho(M_{K_\Delta}^\mu + M_A^\mu L_x), \\
    M_{A_{2,R}}^\mu & := \rho\left[(M_{K_\Delta}^\mu + M_A^\mu L_x)^2 + 2L_x + \frac{1}{4}M_A^\mu L_x^2\right], \\
    M_{B_{2,R}}^\mu & := 2L_x^3 + \frac{1}{4}M_B^\mu L_x^4,\\
    M_{H_{2,R}}^\mu & := \frac{M_{A_{2,R}}^\mu H_{R_\mu} + 2M_{A_{1,R}}^\mu J_{R_\mu} + M_{B_{2,R}}^\mu}{1-\rho}, \\
    L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu} & := H_\ell L_x^2(H_{R_\mu} + M_C^\mu J_{R_\mu})^2 + L_\ell L_x(M_C^\mu M_{H_{2,R}}^\mu + 2J_{R_\mu} ), \label{eq:fedprox_L_SSM_R}\\
    G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu} & := L_\ell L_x(H_{R_\mu}  + M_C^\mu J_{R_\mu}), \label{eq:fedprox_G_SSM_R}
\end{align}
and in addition define:
\begin{equation}
    L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu} = L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}+\mu\label{eq:fedprox_L_prox_R}
\end{equation}

On this neighborhood, Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_local_smoothness} implies that each $\mathcal L_k$ is $L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}$-smooth.

Then, for any $W,V\in\mathcal N_{R_\mu}(W^r)$, the gradients
of the local proximal objective satisfy:
\begin{align}
    &\biggl\|\bigl(\nabla\mathcal L_k(W)+\mu(W-W^r)\bigr) -\bigl(\nabla\mathcal L_k(V)+\mu(V-W^r)\bigr)\biggr\|_F \nonumber \\
    & \qquad \leq \|\nabla\mathcal L_k(W)-\nabla\mathcal L_k(V)\|_F + \mu\|W-V\|_F \nonumber \\ 
    & \qquad \leq  \left(L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}+\mu\right)\left\|W-V\right\|_F \nonumber \\ & \qquad = L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}\|W-V\|_F.\label{eq:prox_smoothness_equation}
\end{align}
Therefore, the local proximal objective $\mathcal L_k(W)+\frac{\mu}{2}\|W - W^r\|_F^2$ is $L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}$-smooth on $\mathcal N_{R_\mu}(W^r)$. Moreover, since $f = \sum_kp_k\mathcal L_k$ with $p_k\geq 0$ and $\sum_k p_k = 1$, the global objective $f$ remains $L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}$-smooth on the same neighborhood.

Next, define the following common deterministic variance constant:
\begin{equation}
    \sigma^2_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu} := \frac{G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2}{\Xi}. \label{eq:prox_sigma_Gamma_mu_bounds}
\end{equation}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_gamma_tv_bound}, for every round $r<T$,
\begin{equation}
    \sup_{W\in\mathcal N_{R_\mu}(W^r)} \sum_k p_k \|\nabla\mathcal L_k(W)-\nabla f(W)\|_F^2 \leq 4G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}. \label{eq:fedprox_uniform_heterogeneity}
\end{equation}
The right-hand side provides a common deterministic heterogeneity bound across rounds.

Next, fix a round $r < T$ and let $\mathbb E_r[\cdot]$ denote an expectation conditional on the training history at the beginning of that round, with $W^r$ being fixed under this conditioning. 

We begin by unrolling the local update and subtracting the starting weights:
\begin{align}
    W_k^{r,e} - W^r &= -\eta \sum_{j=0}^{e-1}\left[g^{r,j}_{k,\Xi}+\mu\left(W^{r,j}_k-W^r\right)\right] \nonumber \\ &= -\eta \sum_{j=0}^{e-1}\left[g^{r,j}_{k,\Xi}+\mu\left(W^{r,j}_k-W^r\right)\right] \nonumber \\ &\quad+ \eta\sum_{j=0}^{e-1}\left(\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^r\right)-\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^{r,j}_k\right)\right) - \eta\sum_{j=0}^{e-1}\left(\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^r\right)-\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^{r,j}_k\right)\right) \nonumber \\
    &= - \eta e\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^r\right) - \eta\sum_{j=0}^{e-1}\left[g^{r,j}_{k,\Xi}-\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^{r,j}_k\right)\right] \nonumber \\ 
    &\quad-\eta \sum_{j=0}^{e-1}\left[\mu\left(W^{r,j}_k-W^r\right)-\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^r\right) + \nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^{r,j}_k\right)\right].\label{eq:fedprox_proof_unrolled_local_model_update}
\end{align}

Next, when evaluating the norms of the individual quantities on the right of~\eqref{eq:fedprox_proof_unrolled_local_model_update}, by Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_variance_bound}, the following holds
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb E_r \left[\left\|\sum_{j=0}^{e-1}\left(g_{k, \Xi}^{r,j}-\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W_k^{r,j}\right)\right)\right\|_F^2\right] \leq e\sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\label{eq:fedprox_proof_diff_stochastic_grad_grad}
\end{equation}
for all $0 \leq e \leq E$. 

Then, by substituting $W_{k}^{r,j}$ and $W^r$ into~\eqref{eq:prox_smoothness_equation}, it follows that:
\begin{equation}
    \left\|\mu\left(W^{r,j}_k-W^r\right)-\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^r\right) + \nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^{r,j}_k\right)\right\|_F \leq L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}\left\|W^{r,j}_k-W^r\right\|_F
\end{equation}

Taking the norm of~\eqref{eq:fedprox_proof_unrolled_local_model_update}, applying the squared-normed inequality for a sum of three terms as well as the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and substituting these results, we get:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F^2\right] &\leq 3\eta^2 e^2 \left\|\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^r\right)\right\|_F^2 + 3\eta^2 e\sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2 \nonumber \\ &\quad + 3\eta^2 e L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2 \sum_{j=0}^{e-1}\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W^{r,j}_k-W^r\right\|_F^2\right].\label{eq:fedprox_proof_cond_exp_model_diff_bound_init}
\end{align}

By multiplying~\eqref{eq:fedprox_proof_cond_exp_model_diff_bound_init} by $p_k$ and summing over all clients, we get:
\begin{align}
    \sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F^2\right] &\leq 3\eta^2 e\sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2 + 3\eta^2 e^2 \sum_{k=1}^K p_k\left\|\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^r\right)\right\|_F^2  \nonumber \\ &\quad + 3\eta^2 e L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2 \sum_{j=0}^{e-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W^{r,j}_k-W^r\right\|_F^2\right].\label{eq:fedprox_proof_cond_exp_model_diff_bound_clients_averaged}
\end{align}

Next, note that:
\begin{align}
    \sum_{k=1}^K p_k\left\|\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^r\right)\right\|_F^2 &= \sum_{k=1}^K p_k\left\|\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^r\right) +\nabla f(W^r)-\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 \nonumber \\ 
    &\leq \left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2+ \sum_{k=1}^K p_k\left\|\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^r\right)-\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 \nonumber \\
    &\leq \left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 + 4G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}.
\end{align}
where the last inequality follows from~\eqref{eq:fedprox_uniform_heterogeneity}. Substituting this result into~\eqref{eq:fedprox_proof_cond_exp_model_diff_bound_clients_averaged}, we get:
\begin{align}
    \sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F^2\right] &\leq 3\eta^2 e\sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2 + 3\eta^2 e^2\left(\left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 + 4G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}\right)
    \nonumber \\ &\quad + 3\eta^2 e L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2 \sum_{j=0}^{e-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W^{r,j}_k-W^r\right\|_F^2\right].\label{eq:fedprox_proof_cond_exp_model_diff_bound_clients_averaged_with_global_gradient}
\end{align}

Averaging~\eqref{eq:fedprox_proof_cond_exp_model_diff_bound_clients_averaged_with_global_gradient} over $e=0, \dots, E-1$, where we utilize $\frac{1}{E}\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}e = \frac{E-1}{2}$ and $\frac{1}{E}\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}e^2 \leq E(E-1)$, yields:
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{E}\sum_{e=1}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F^2\right] &\leq \frac{3}{2}\eta^2(E-1)\sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2 \nonumber \\ &\quad + 3\eta^2E(E-1)\left(\left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 + 4G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}\right)
    \nonumber \\ &\quad + 3\eta^2 E(E-1) L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2 \left[\frac{1}{E}\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W^{r,e}_k-W^r\right\|_F^2\right]\right],\label{eq:fedprox_proof_cond_exp_model_diff_bound_clients_averaged_with_global_gradient_rounds_averaged}
\end{align}
where for the last term, we used:
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{E}\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}e\sum_{j=0}^{e-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W^{r,j}_k-W^r\right\|_F^2\right] \leq E(E-1) \left[\frac{1}{E}\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W^{r,e}_k-W^r\right\|_F^2\right] \right]
\end{align}

Rearranging the terms in~\eqref{eq:fedprox_proof_cond_exp_model_diff_bound_clients_averaged_with_global_gradient_rounds_averaged} and dividing out by $1-3\eta^2 E(E-1) L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2$, yields:
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{E}\sum_{e=1}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F^2\right] &\leq \frac{1}{1-3\eta^2 E(E-1) L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2}\Bigg(\frac{3}{2}\eta^2(E-1)\sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2 \nonumber \\ &\quad + 3\eta^2E(E-1)\left(\left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 + 4G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}\right)\Bigg).\label{eq:fedprox_proof_inequality_difference_rearanged}
\end{align}
Next, note that by the step-size condition $12\eta^2 E(E-1) L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2 \leq 1$, it is true that $3\eta^2 E(E-1) L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2 \leq \frac{1}{4}$, and therefore $\frac{1}{1-3\eta^2 E(E-1) L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2} \leq \frac{4}{3}$. Substituting that result into~\eqref{eq:fedprox_proof_inequality_difference_rearanged} yields:
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{E}\sum_{e=1}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F^2\right] &\leq 2\eta^2(E-1)\sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2 \nonumber \\ &\quad + 4\eta^2E(E-1)\left(\left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 + 4G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}\right).\label{eq:fedprox_proof_final_inequality_local_model_differnce}
\end{align}

Next, we evaluate $W^{r+1}-W^r$. By the update rule:
\begin{equation}
    W^{r+1}-W^r = -\eta \sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k\left[g^{r,e}_{k,\Xi}+\mu\left(W^{r,e}_k-W^r\right)\right].
\end{equation}

Since the segment joining $W^r$ and $W^{r+1}$ lies in $\mathcal N_{R_\mu}(W^r)$, by the smoothness of $f$ established in Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_local_smoothness}, it is true that:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb E_r\left[f(W^{r+1})\right] \leq& f(W^r) \nonumber \\ &-\eta\left\langle \nabla f\left(W^r\right), \mathbb E_r \left[\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k \left(\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^{r,e}_k\right)+\mu \left(W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right)\right)\right]\right\rangle_F \nonumber \\ &+ \frac{\eta^2L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}}{2}\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k\left(g^{r,e}_{k,\Xi}+\mu\left(W^{r,e}_k-W^r\right)\right)\right\|_F^2\right].\label{eq:fedprox_proof_expected_next_round_loss_init}
\end{align}
Here, the tower property and because of conditional unbiasedness, the stochastic gradients can be replaced by local gradients inside the expected inner product. 

Next, note that:
\begin{align}
   \sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k\left(g^{r,e}_{k,\Xi}+\mu\left(W^{r,e}_k-W^r\right)\right) &= \sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k\left(g^{r,e}_{k,\Xi}+\mu\left(W^{r,e}_k-W^r\right)+\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W_{k}^{r,e}\right)-\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W_{k}^{r,e}\right)\right) \nonumber \\ &= \sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k\left(\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W_{k}^{r,e}\right)+\mu\left(W^{r,e}_k-W^r\right)\right) \nonumber \\ &\quad+\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k\left(g^{r,e}_{k,\Xi}-\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W_{k}^{r,e}\right)\right).
\end{align}
When taking norms, for a sum of two matrices, the squared norm is at most twice the sum of their squared norms. As such, it follows that:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb E_r\left[\left\|\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k\left(g^{r,e}_{k,\Xi}+\mu\left(W^{r,e}_k-W^r\right)\right)\right\|_F^2\right] &\leq 2\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k\left(g^{r,e}_{k,\Xi}-\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W_{k}^{r,e}\right)\right)\right\|_F^2\right] \nonumber \\ &\quad + 2\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k\left(\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W_{k}^{r,e}\right)+\mu\left(W^{r,e}_k-W^r\right)\right)\right\|_F^2\right]
\end{align}

Next, the centered gradient-noise differences have conditional mean zero given the complete history before each local step. Conditional independence across clients eliminates the same-step cross terms, while conditional unbiasedness and the tower property eliminate the cross terms between different local steps. Applying Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_variance_bound} therefore gives
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb E_r\left[\left\|\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k\left(g^{r,e}_{k,\Xi}-\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W_{k}^{r,e}\right)\right)\right\|_F^2\right] \leq \sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k^2  \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2 =E\sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k^2,
\end{equation}
and as a result:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb E_r\left[\left\|\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k\left(g^{r,e}_{k,\Xi}+\mu\left(W^{r,e}_k-W^r\right)\right)\right\|_F^2\right] &\leq 2E\sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k^2 \nonumber \\ &\quad + 2\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k\left(\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W_{k}^{r,e}\right)+\mu\left(W^{r,e}_k-W^r\right)\right)\right\|_F^2\right]
\end{align}

When it comes to the inner-product term, we note that, since $f(W^r) = \sum_{k=1}^Kp_k \mathcal L_{k}(W^r)$, it is true that:
\begin{equation}
    E \nabla f(W^r)=\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k \nabla \mathcal L_k(W^r).
\end{equation}

Using this result, and the $-2\langle U,V\rangle_F=\|U-V\|_F^2-\|U\|_F^2-\|V\|_F^2$ equality, the following holds:
\begin{align}
    -&\eta\left\langle \nabla f\left(W^r\right), \mathbb E_r \left[\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k \left(\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^{r,e}_k\right)+\mu \left(W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right)\right)\right]\right\rangle_F \\ &= -\frac{\eta E}{2}\left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 \nonumber \\ &-\frac{\eta}{2E}\mathbb E_r \left[\left\|\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k \left(\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^{r,e}_k\right)+\mu \left(W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right)\right)\right\|_F^2\right] \nonumber \\ &+\frac{\eta}{2E}\mathbb E_r \left[\left\|\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k \left(\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^{r,e}_k\right)+\mu \left(W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right)-\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^r\right)\right)\right\|_F^2\right],
\end{align}
where the $E$-terms appeared from evaluating the inner-product with $E \nabla f(W^r)$ and dividing out by $E$.

Then, the positive term is bounded as:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb E_r &\left[\left\|\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k \left(\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^{r,e}_k\right)+\mu \left(W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right)-\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^r\right)\right)\right\|_F^2\right] \nonumber \\
    &\leq E\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k \mathbb E_r \left[\left\|\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^{r,e}_k\right)+\mu \left(W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right)-\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^r\right)\right\|_F^2\right] \nonumber \\ &\leq E L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2 \sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k \mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W_k^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F^2\right]
\end{align}
where the first inequality is true by Jensen's inequality with weights $p_k/E$, and the second inequality follows from~\eqref{eq:prox_smoothness_equation}.

Substituting these results back into~\eqref{eq:fedprox_proof_expected_next_round_loss_init}, we obtain:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb E_r\left[f\left(W^{r+1}\right)\right] \leq& f\left(W^r\right) -\frac{\eta E}{2}\left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 \nonumber \\
    &+ \left(\eta^2 L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}-\frac{\eta}{2E} \right)\mathbb E_r \left[\left\|\sum_{e=0}^{E-1}\sum_{k=1}^K p_k \left(\nabla \mathcal L_k\left(W^{r,e}_k\right)+\mu \left(W_{k}^{r,e}-W^r\right)\right)\right\|_F^2\right] \nonumber \\
    &+ \frac{\eta L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2 }{2}\sum_{e=0}^{E-1} \sum_{k=1}^K p_k\mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W_k^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F^2\right] \nonumber \\ 
    &+ \eta^2 EL_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu} \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k^2.
\end{align}

The step-size condition $\eta E L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}\leq 1/2$ implies that $\eta^2 L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}-\frac{\eta}{2E} \leq 0$, and therefore the term multiplied by this factor is never positive. As such, the following holds:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb E_r\left[f\left(W^{r+1}\right)\right] \leq& f\left(W^r\right) -\frac{\eta E}{2}\left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 \nonumber \\
    &+ \frac{\eta L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2 }{2} \sum_{e=0}^{E-1} \sum_{k=1}^{K}  p_k \mathbb E_r\left[\left\|W_k^{r,e}-W^r\right\|_F^2\right] \nonumber \\ 
    &+ \eta^2 EL_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu} \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k^2.
\end{align}

Substituting the result from~\eqref{eq:fedprox_proof_final_inequality_local_model_differnce} into this inequality, we get:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb E_r\left[f\left(W^{r+1}\right)\right] \leq& f\left(W^r\right) \nonumber \\ &+ \left(2\eta^3E^2(E-1)L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2 -\frac{\eta E}{2}\right)\left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 \nonumber \\
    &+ \eta^3 E(E-1) L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2  \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2 \nonumber \\ &+ 8\eta^3E^2(E-1)L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}\nonumber  \\ 
    &+ \eta^2 EL_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu} \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k^2.\label{eq:fedprox_proof_one_round_descent_init}
\end{align}

Next, by the second step size condition $12\eta^2L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2E(E-1) \leq 1$ it follows that:
\begin{align}
    2\eta^3E^2(E-1)L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2 -\frac{\eta E}{2} &= \eta E\left(2\eta^2 E(E-1)L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2 - \frac{1}{2}\right) \nonumber \\ &\leq \eta E \left(\frac{1}{6}-\frac{1}{2}\right) = -\frac{\eta E}{3}.
\end{align}
Substituting in~\eqref{eq:fedprox_proof_one_round_descent_init}, we get:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb E_r\left[f\left(W^{r+1}\right)\right] \leq& f\left(W^r\right) \nonumber \\ &-\frac{\eta E}{3}\left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 \nonumber \\
    &+ \eta^3 E(E-1) L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2  \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2 \nonumber \\ &+ 8\eta^3E^2(E-1)L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}\nonumber  \\ 
    &+ \eta^2 EL_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu} \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k^2.\label{eq:fedprox_proof_one_round_descent}
\end{align}

Taking total expectations in~ \eqref{eq:fedprox_proof_one_round_descent}, rearranging, and summing over $r=0,\ldots,T-1$ makes the objective differences telescope. Using $\mathbb E[f(W^T)]\geq f^*$ and dividing by $\eta ET/3$, we obtain:
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{T}\sum_{r=0}^{T-1}\mathbb E\left\|\nabla f(W^r)\right\|_F^2 \leq & \frac{3\left(f(W^0)-f^*\right)}{\eta ET} + 3\eta L_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu} \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\sum_{k=1}^Kp_k^2 \nonumber \\
    & + 3\eta^2 (E-1) L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2  \sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2 + 24\eta^2E(E-1)L_{\mathrm{prox},R_\mu}^2G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2\mathcal H_{\mathrm{TV}}.\label{eq:fedprox_proof_final_inequality_with_sigma}
\end{align}

Finally, substituting $\sigma_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2
=G_{\mathrm{SSM},R_\mu}^2/\Xi$ (which follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:ssm_variance_bound}) into~\eqref{eq:fedprox_proof_final_inequality_with_sigma}, gives the stated bound, completing the proof. \qed
<!-- end head-7 -->
<!-- assumption-4: body_md -->
\label{assum:bounded_layer_inuts}
For every layer $q\in \{1, \dots, Q\}$ of a multi-layer selective SSM of depth $Q$ parameterized by a fixed set of weights $W=\left(W^{(1)},\dots, W^{(Q)}\right)$, there exists a constant $L_{x,q} > 0$ such that:
\begin{equation}
    \left\|x_t^{(q)}\left(W;\xi\right)\right\|_2 \leq L_{x,q} \text{ } \forall t\in \{1, \dots, S\}.
\end{equation}
This holds for every finite integer length $S \geq 1$, every admissible input-target pair $\xi \in \mathcal Z$, and every token index $t \in \{1, \dots, S\}$, with finite constants independent of $S$, $\xi$, and $t$.
<!-- end assumption-4 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:multilayer_ssm_stability}
For each layer $q \in \left\{1, \dots, Q\right\}$, let $\gamma_q > 0$ and $\delta_q > 0$. We define ${\mathcal S}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}, \boldsymbol{\delta}}^{(Q)}$ as follows:
\begin{align}
   {\mathcal S}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}, \boldsymbol{\delta}}^{(Q)} := \bigg\{ &W \bigg | A_n^{{(q)}} \leq -\gamma_q, \left[\operatorname{Softplus}\left(W_{\Delta}^{(q)}x_{t}^{(q)}\left(W;\xi\right)\right)\right]_d \geq \delta_q \nonumber \\ &\forall q\in\{1,\dots,Q\}, \forall n\in\{1,\dots,N\} ,\forall d\in\{1,\dots,D\}, \forall t\in\{1,\dots,S\}, \nonumber \\ &\text{finite integer } S \geq 1, \text{and every admissible length}-S \text{ pair } \xi \in \mathcal Z \bigg\},\label{eq:S_Q_gamma_delta}
\end{align}
where $\boldsymbol{\gamma} = \left(\gamma_1, \dots, \gamma_Q\right)$ and $\boldsymbol{\delta} = \left(\delta_1, \dots, \delta_Q\right)$

We then say that the full parameter tuple $W$ is stable with these margins if $W \in{\mathcal S}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}, \boldsymbol{\delta}}^{(Q)}$.

For each layer $q$, we further define:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rho_q}
    \rho_q := \exp(-\gamma_q\delta_q) < 1.
\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:ssm_multi_layer_grad_bound}
Let $W = \left(W^{(1)}, \dots, W^{(Q)}\right)$ be a full parameter tuple satisfying Assumptions~\ref{assum:lipschitz_cont_loss} and~\ref{assum:bounded_layer_inuts}, with $W \in{\mathcal S}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}, \boldsymbol{\delta}}^{(Q)}$, that parametrizes a multi-layer selective SSM model of finite integer depth $Q \geq 1$. Then, for such $W$, for every finite integer $S \geq 1$ and every admissible input-target pair $\xi \in \mathcal Z$ of length $S$, the following bound holds:
\begin{equation}
    \textstyle \left\|\nabla_W \mathcal L^{S,(Q)}(W;\xi)\right\|_F \leq  L_\ell \left  (\sum_{q=1}^Q L_{x,q}^2\left(H_{q} + \left\|W_C^{(q)}\right\|_2J_{q}\right)^2 \prod _{q'=q+1}^Q \kappa_{q'}^2\right)^{1/2} := \mathcal G_{\text{SSM}}^{(Q)}(W)
\end{equation}
with
\begin{align}
    K_{\Delta, q} &:= \left\|W_\Delta^{(q)}\right\|_2L_{x, q} + \sqrt{D}\log(2), 
    ~~~~ K_{B,q} := \left\|W_B^{(q)}\right\|_2 L_{x,q}^2, \\ 
    K_{W,q} &:= ND K_{B,q}K_{\Delta,q} \left(K_{\Delta,q} + \sqrt{D} \left\|A^{(q)}\right\|_2L_{x,q}\right)
    \left(\frac{\rho_q}{1-\rho_q}\right) + L_{x,q}^2 \sqrt{N} K_{\Delta,q} + L_{x,q} \sqrt{D} K_{B,q},\\
    H_q &:= \frac{K_{B,q} K_{\Delta,q}
    \sqrt{ND}}{1-\rho_q} , ~~~~ J_{q}:= \frac{K_{W,q}}{1-\rho_q}, ~~~~ \kappa_{q}:=\left\|W_C^{(q)}\right\|_2\left(H_{{q}}+ \frac{L_{x,q}\Lambda_{x,q}}{1-\rho_{q}}\right), \\ 
    \Lambda_{x,q} &:= \rho_{q} \left\| A^{(q)}\right\|_2 \left\|W_\Delta^{(q)}\right\|_2 H_{{q}} + \left\|W_B^{(q)}\right\|_2  L_{x,q} \left(2K_{\Delta,q} + L_{x,q}\left\|W_\Delta^{(q)}\right\|_2\right).
\end{align}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- head-8: body_md -->
Fix an arbitrary full parameter tuple $W$ satisfying Assumptions~\ref{assum:lipschitz_cont_loss} and~\ref{assum:bounded_layer_inuts} such that $W \in{\mathcal S}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}, \boldsymbol{\delta}}^{(Q)}$. Let $\xi \in \mathcal Z$ be some fixed admissible sequence pair of length $S$ ($S \geq 1$ is some fixed finite integer). 

By the definition of a gradient with respect to a tuple in equation~\eqref{eq:def_func_gradient}, it follows that:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:desired_bound_on_seq_S}
    \left\|\nabla_W \mathcal L^{S, (Q)}(W)\right\|_F = \left(\sum_{q=1}^Q \left\|\nabla_{W^{(q)}} \mathcal L^{S, (Q)}(W)\right\|_F^2\right)^{1/2}
\end{equation}

As a result, to derive a bound on the gradient norm with respect to the full model parameter tuple $W$, it is sufficient to derive the bound on:
\begin{equation}
    \left\|\nabla_{W^{(q)}} \mathcal L^{S, (Q)}(W)\right\|_F^2
\end{equation}
for an arbitrary layer $q\in\{1,\dots,Q\}$, and combine the resulting layer-wise bounds. 

This gradient can be expanded as:
\begin{equation}
    \nabla_{W^{(q)}} \mathcal L^{S, (Q)}(W) = \nabla_{W^{(q)}}\left(\frac{1}{S}\sum_{t=1}^S \ell_t\left(y_t^{(Q)}\right)\right) = \frac{1}{S}\sum_{t=1}^S\nabla_{W^{(q)}} \ell_t\left(y_{t}^{(Q)}\right)
\end{equation}

Applying the chain rule again, we get: 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multi_layer_loss_gradient_expression}
    \operatorname{vec}\left(\nabla_{W^{(q)}}\mathcal L^{S,(Q)}(W)\right) =\frac{1}{S}\sum_{t=1}^S\left(\frac{\partial y_t^{(Q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}\right)^\top\nabla_y\ell_t\left(y_t^{(Q)}\right).
\end{equation}

For $Q\geq2$, we first consider the case $q=Q-1$ and focus on $\partial y_t^{(Q)}/\partial W^{(q)}$, since $\nabla_y\ell_t\left(y_t^{(Q)}\right)$ is independent of $q$. By the product rule and using the SSM dynamics, it follows that:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multi_layer_dyQ_dWq_less_1}
    \frac{\partial y_t^{(Q)}}{\partial W^{(Q-1)}} = \frac{\partial \left(h_t^{(Q)}\right)^\top}{\partial W^{{(Q-1)}}}C_t^{(Q)}+\left(h_t^{(Q)}\right)^\top \frac{\partial C_t^{(Q)}}{\partial W^{(Q-1)}}
\end{equation}

Next, we evaluate~\eqref{eq:multi_layer_dyQ_dWq_less_1} coordinate-wise, as this avoids any ambiguity associated with calculating the matrix-valued derivatives with respect to the parameter tuples. Let $w^{(Q-1)}$ denote an arbitrary scalar coordinate of $W^{(Q-1)}$, and let $d \in \{1, \dots, D\}$ denote an arbitrary coordinate of $y^{(Q)}_t$. Then:
\begin{equation}
    y_{t,d}^{(Q)} = \sum_{n=1}^N h_{t,n,d}^{(Q)} C_{t,n}^{(Q)},
\end{equation}
and by the product rule:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multi_layer_dyQ_dWq_less_1_coordinate}
    \frac{\partial y_{t,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}} = \sum_{n=1}^N \left[C_{t,n}^{(Q)} \frac{\partial h_{t,n,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}} + h_{t,n,d}^{(Q)}\frac{\partial C_{t,n}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}}\right]
\end{equation}

Next, since the weight $w^{(Q-1)}$ is not a parameter of layer $Q$, it influences $h_t^{(Q)}$ at any coordinate only through the inputs to layer $Q$ (i.e. $h_t^{(Q)}$ is a function of all  $x^{(Q)}_1, \dots, x^{(Q)}_j, \dots, x^{(Q)}_t$, which depend on the parameters of the previous layer through $x^{(Q)}_j=y^{(Q-1)}_j$). As a result:
\begin{equation}
    \frac{\partial h_{t,n,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}} = \sum_{j=1}^t\sum_{d'=1}^D \frac{\partial h_{t,n,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial x_{j,d'}^{(Q)}} \frac{\partial x_{j,d'}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}}.
\end{equation}

Furthermore, since $C_{t,n}^{(Q)} = \sum_{d'=1}^D \left(W_C^{(Q)}\right)_{n, d'} x_{t, d'}^{(Q)}$, and $W_C^{(Q)}$ is independent of any parameter of $W^{(Q-1)}$, it follows that:
\begin{equation}
    \frac{\partial C_{t,n}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}} = \sum_{d'=1}^D \left(W_C^{(Q)}\right)_{n, d'} \frac{\partial x_{t, d'}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}}
\end{equation}

Substituting these expressions into~\eqref{eq:multi_layer_dyQ_dWq_less_1_coordinate} results in:
\begin{align}
    \frac{\partial y_{t,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}} &= \sum_{n=1}^N \left[C_{t,n}^{(Q)}\sum_{j=1}^t\sum_{d'=1}^D \frac{\partial h_{t,n,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial x_{j,d'}^{(Q)}} \frac{\partial x_{j,d'}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}} + h_{t,n,d}^{(Q)}\sum_{d'=1}^D \left(W_C^{(Q)}\right)_{n, d'} \frac{\partial x_{t, d'}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}}\right] \nonumber \\ &= \sum_{n=1}^N \left[C_{t,n}^{(Q)}\sum_{j=1}^t\sum_{d'=1}^D \frac{\partial h_{t,n,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial x_{j,d'}^{(Q)}} \frac{\partial x_{j,d'}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}} + h_{t,n,d}^{(Q)}\sum_{j=1}^t \sum_{d'=1}^D \mathbbm{1}_{j=t} \left(W_C^{(Q)}\right)_{n, d'} \frac{\partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}}\right] \nonumber \\ &= \sum_{j=1}^t \sum_{d'=1}^D\left[\sum_{n=1}^N \left( C_{t,n}^{(Q)}\frac{\partial h_{t,n,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial x_{j,d'}^{(Q)}} + \mathbbm{1}_{j=t} h_{t, n, d}^{(Q)} \left(W_C^{(Q)}\right)_{n, d'}\right) \right]\frac{\partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}}
\end{align}
where $\mathbbm{1}_{j=t}$ is $1$ if $j =t$ and $0$ otherwise. 

Next, we note that:
\begin{align}
    \frac{\partial y_{t, d}^{(Q)}}{ \partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}} &=   \frac{\partial \left(\sum_{n=1}^N h_{t, n, d}^{(Q)} C_{t, n}^{(Q)}\right)}{ \partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}} =  \sum_{n=1}^N \frac{\partial \left(h_{t, n, d}^{(Q)} C_{t, n}^{(Q)}\right)}{\partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}} = \sum_{n=1}^N \left[C_{t, n}^{(Q)} \frac{\partial h_{t, n, d}^{(Q)}}{\partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}} + h_{t, n, d}^{(Q)} \frac{\partial C_{t, n}^{(Q)}}{\partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}}\right] \nonumber \\ &= \sum_{n=1}^N \left[C_{t, n}^{(Q)} \frac{\partial h_{t, n, d}^{(Q)}}{\partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}} + h_{t, n, d}^{(Q)} \frac{\partial \left(\sum_{d''=1}^D \left(W_C^{(Q)}\right)_{n, d''} x_{t, d''}^{(Q)}\right)}{\partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}}\right] \nonumber \\ &= \sum_{n=1}^N \left( C_{t,n}^{(Q)}\frac{\partial h_{t,n,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial x_{j,d'}^{(Q)}} + \mathbbm{1}_{j=t} h_{t, n, d}^{(Q)} \left(W_C^{(Q)}\right)_{n, d'}\right) 
\end{align}
where we use the fact that if $j \neq t$, 
\begin{equation}
    \frac{\partial \left(\sum_{d''=1}^D \left(W_C^{(Q)}\right)_{n, d''} x_{t, d''}^{(Q)}\right)}{\partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}} = 0
\end{equation}
and if $j=t$, 
\begin{equation}
    \frac{\partial \left(\sum_{d''=1}^D \left(W_C^{(Q)}\right)_{n, d''} x_{j, d''}^{(Q)}\right)}{\partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}} =  \left(W_C^{(Q)}\right)_{n, d'}
\end{equation}

As a result, it follows that:
\begin{equation}
    \frac{\partial y_{t,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}} = \sum_{j=1}^t \sum_{d'=1}^D\frac{\partial y_{t, d}^{(Q)}}{ \partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}} \frac{\partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}}
\end{equation}
and finally, since $x_{j, d'}^{(Q)} = y_{j, d'}^{(Q-1)}$, 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:recursion_multi_layer_coordinate_less_1}
    \frac{\partial y_{t,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}} = \sum_{j=1}^t \sum_{d'=1}^D\frac{\partial y_{t, d}^{(Q)}}{ \partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}} \frac{\partial y_{j, d'}^{(Q-1)}}{\partial w^{(Q-1)}}
\end{equation}

For $Q \geq 3$, the same coordinate-wise chain rule argument applies to $w^{(Q-2)}$. That is, since $w^{(Q-2)}$ is not a parameter of layer $Q$, it influences $y^{(Q)}_t$ only through the inputs sequence $x_1^{(Q)}, \dots, x_t^{(Q)}$. As such, it follows that:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:recursion_multi_layer_coordinate_less_2}
    \frac{\partial y_{t,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-2)}} = \sum_{j=1}^t \sum_{d'=1}^D\frac{\partial y_{t, d}^{(Q)}}{ \partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}} \frac{\partial y_{j, d'}^{(Q-1)}}{\partial w^{(Q-2)}}
\end{equation}

Furthermore, using the same argument applied to layer $Q-1$, we can say that:
\begin{equation}
    \frac{\partial y_{j, d'}^{(Q-1)}}{\partial w^{(Q-2)}} = \sum_{j'=1}^j \sum_{d''=1}^D\frac{\partial y_{j, d'}^{(Q-1)}}{ \partial x_{j', d''}^{(Q-1)}} \frac{\partial y_{j', d''}^{(Q-2)}}{\partial w^{(Q-2)}}.
\end{equation}

Substituting this result into~\eqref{eq:recursion_multi_layer_coordinate_less_2}, we obtain:
\begin{equation}
    \frac{\partial y_{t,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(Q-2)}} = \sum_{j=1}^t \sum_{d'=1}^D\frac{\partial y_{t, d}^{(Q)}}{ \partial x_{j, d'}^{(Q)}} \sum_{j'=1}^j \sum_{d''=1}^D\frac{\partial y_{j, d'}^{(Q-1)}}{ \partial x_{j', d''}^{(Q-1)}} \frac{\partial y_{j', d''}^{(Q-2)}}{\partial w^{(Q-2)}} = \sum_{j=1}^t\sum_{j'=1}^{j} \sum_{d'=1}^D\sum_{d''=1}^D \frac{\partial y_{t,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial x_{j,d'}^{(Q)}} \frac{\partial y_{j,d'}^{(Q-1)}}{\partial x_{j',d''}^{(Q-1)}} \frac{\partial y_{j',d''}^{(Q-2)}}{\partial w^{(Q-2)}}
\end{equation}

This result means that, compared to the case of $w^{(Q-1)}$, the derivative with respect to $w^{(Q-2)}$ contains one additional input-output derivative, which corresponds to its propagation through layer $Q-1$. Applying this argument recursively, we derive the final derivative of the $d$-th coordinate of the final output with respect to some parameter $w^{(q)}$ of the layer $q\in\{1, \dots, Q-1\}$ as:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multi_layer_derived_recurrence_coordinate}
    \frac{\partial y_{t,d}^{(Q)}}{\partial w^{(q)}} = \sum_{j_{Q-1}=1}^{j_Q}\cdots\sum_{j_q=1}^{j_{q+1}}\sum_{d_{Q-1}=1}^D\cdots\sum_{d_q=1}^D\left[\prod_{q'=q+1}^{Q}\frac{\partial y_{j_{q'},d_{q'}}^{({q'})}}{\partial x_{j_{{q'}-1},d_{{q'}-1}}^{(q')}}\right] \frac{\partial y_{j_q,d_q}^{(q)}}{\partial w^{(q)}},
\end{equation}
where we also have $j_Q=t$, $d_Q=d$, and $j_q\leq j_{q+1}\leq\cdots\leq j_{Q-1}\leq j_Q=t$.

We next collect the coordinate derivatives in~\eqref{eq:multi_layer_derived_recurrence_coordinate} into matrices, which transforms sums over feature coordinates into matrix products, while the sums over time indices remain explicit. 

For each layer $q$, define the input-output Jacobian matrix as:
\begin{equation}
    \mathbf{K}_{t,j}^{(q)} := \frac{\partial y_t^{(q)}}{\partial x_j^{(q)}} = \begin{pmatrix}
        \dfrac{\partial y_{t,1}^{(q)}}{\partial x_{j,1}^{(q)}} & \cdots & \dfrac{\partial y_{t,1}^{(q)}}{\partial x_{j,D}^{(q)}} \\ \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ \dfrac{\partial y_{t,D}^{(q)}}{\partial x_{j,1}^{(q)}} & \dots & \dfrac{\partial y_{t,D}^{(q)}}{\partial x_{j,D}^{(q)}} 
    \end{pmatrix} \in \mathbb R^{D \times D}.
\end{equation}
The Jacobian is computed with the parameters of layer $q$ and all input tokens other than $x_j^{(q)}$ held fixed, and evaluated at the input sequence received by that layer. Additionally, by causality, $\mathbf{K}_{t,j}^{(q)} = \mathbf{0}_{D\times D}$ for $j > t$.

Then, for any layer $q\in \{1, \dots, Q-1\}$, for any subsequent layer $q' \in \{q+1, \dots, Q\}$, the parameters $W^{(q)}$ affect $y^{(q')}$ only through the input sequence to layer $q'$. Since $x_j^{(q')} = y_j^{(q'-1)}$, the chain rule can be written in the matrix form as:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multilayer_jacobian_recursion}
     \frac{\partial y_t^{(q')}}{\partial W^{(q)}} = \sum_{j=1}^t \frac{\partial y_t^{(q')}}{\partial x_j^{(q')}}\frac{\partial x_j^{(q')}}{\partial W^{(q)}} = \sum_{j=1}^t \mathbf K_{t,j}^{(q')}\frac{\partial y_j^{(q'-1)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}.
\end{equation}

Applying this recursively, we get:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multi_layer_derived_recurrence_matrix}
    \frac{\partial y_t^{(Q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}} = \sum_{j_{Q-1}=1}^{j_Q}\cdots\sum_{j_q=1}^{j_{q+1}} \left[\mathbf{K}_{j_Q, j_{Q-1}}^{(Q)} \times  \cdots \times \mathbf{K}_{j_{q+1}, j_{q}}^{(q+1)} \right] \frac{\partial y_{j_q}^{(q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}
\end{equation}
for any $q\in \{1, \dots, Q-1\}$, the matrix product contains only $\mathbf{K}_{t, j_{Q-1}}^{(Q)}$ for the case $q = Q-1$, the $\times$ operator denotes matrix multiplication, and $j_Q=t$. 

Note that equation~\eqref{eq:multi_layer_derived_recurrence_matrix} provides an explicit expression for the parameter Jacobian $\frac{\partial y_t^{(Q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}$ appearing in~\eqref{eq:multi_layer_loss_gradient_expression}, in terms of the own-layer parameter Jacobians $\frac{\partial y_{j_q}^{(q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}$ and the input-output Jacobians of the subsequent layers. 

We next use this decomposition to bound the norm of the parameter Jacobian. We use the Frobenius norm $\|\cdot\|_F$ for the flattened parameter Jacobians and the spectral norm $\|\cdot\|_2$ (where for $M \in \mathbb R^{D \times D}$, $\|M\|_2 := \sup_{\substack{v \in \mathbb R^D \\ \|v\|_2=1}}\|Mv\|_2$) for the input-output matrices. Applying the triangle inequality and the fact that $\|AB\|_F \leq \|A\|_2 \|B\|_F$, it follows that:
\begin{align}
    \left\|\frac{\partial y_t^{(Q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}\right\|_F &= \left\|\sum_{j_{Q-1}=1}^{j_Q}\cdots\sum_{j_q=1}^{j_{q+1}} \left[\mathbf{K}_{j_Q, j_{Q-1}}^{(Q)} \times  \cdots \times \mathbf{K}_{j_{q+1}, j_{q}}^{(q+1)} \right] \frac{\partial y_{j_q}^{(q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}\right\|_F \nonumber \\ &\leq \sum_{j_{Q-1}=1}^{j_Q}\cdots\sum_{j_q=1}^{j_{q+1}}\left\|\left[\mathbf{K}_{j_Q, j_{Q-1}}^{(Q)} \times  \cdots \times \mathbf{K}_{j_{q+1}, j_{q}}^{(q+1)} \right] \frac{\partial y_{j_q}^{(q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}\right\|_F \nonumber \\
    & \leq \sum_{j_{Q-1}=1}^{j_Q}\cdots\sum_{j_q=1}^{j_{q+1}}\left[ \prod_{q'=q+1}^{Q} \left\|\mathbf{K}_{j_{q'}, j_{q'-1}}^{(q')}\right\|_2\right]\left\|\frac{\partial y_{j_q}^{(q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}\right\|_F, \label{eq:output_jacobian_norm_bound}
\end{align}
where $j_Q=t$ and $q\in \{1, \dots, Q-1\}$. 

Next, we bound the terms of this expression separately. Thanks to Assumption~\ref{assum:bounded_layer_inuts} and Definition~\ref{def:multilayer_ssm_stability}, we can apply the single-layer gradient proof argument from Appendix~\ref{appendix:gradient_norm_bound_proof} to each layer $q$, bounding the term $\left\|\frac{\partial y_{j_q}^{(q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}\right\|_F$.

First, for each $q\in \{1, \dots, Q\}$, let:
\begin{align}
    K_{\Delta, q} &:= \left\|W_\Delta^{(q)}\right\|_2L_{x, q} + \sqrt{D}\log(2), ~~~~
    K_{B,q} := \left\|W_B^{(q)}\right\|_2 L_{x,q}^2, \\ K_{W,q} &:= ND K_{B,q}K_{\Delta,q} \left(K_{\Delta,q} + \sqrt{D} \left\|A^{(q)}\right\|_2L_{x,q}\right)
    \left(\frac{\rho_q}{1-\rho_q}\right) + L_{x,q}^2 \sqrt{N} K_{\Delta,q} + L_{x,q} \sqrt{D} K_{B,q}.
\end{align}

Given these definitions, we apply the single-layer argument from Appendix~\ref{appendix:gradient_norm_bound_proof} for every layer $q \in \{1, \dots, Q\}$. Since the input to any layer $q$ depends only on the parameters of the preceding layers, it remains fixed when differentiating with respect to $W^{(q)}$. It therefore follows that:
\begin{align}
    \left\|h_j^{(q)}\right\|_F &\leq \frac{K_{B,q} K_{\Delta,q}
    \sqrt{ND}}{1-\rho_q} := H_{q}, \label{eq:multi_layer_state_bound} \\ \left\|\frac{\partial h_j^{(q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}\right\|_F &\leq \frac{K_{W,q}}{1-\rho_q} := J_{q}, \label{eq:multi_layer_state_jacobian_bound}
\end{align}
for every $q \in \{1, \dots, Q\}$ and $j \in \{1, \dots, S\}$. Here, the state Jacobian is flattened as in the single-layer analysis. 

Then, by definition of $C_j^{(q)}$, Assumption~\ref{assum:bounded_layer_inuts} gives:
\begin{equation}
    \left\|C_j^{(q)}\right\|_2 = \left\|W_C^{(q)} x_j^{(q)}\right\|_2 \leq \left\|W_C^{(q)}\right\|_2 L_{x,q}.
\end{equation}

Finally, by applying the same product-rule argument to $y_j^{(q)} = \left(h_j^{(q)}\right)^\top C_j^{(q)}$ as in Appendix~\ref{appendix:gradient_norm_bound_proof}, and using~\eqref{eq:multi_layer_state_bound} and~\eqref{eq:multi_layer_state_jacobian_bound}, we arrive at:
\begin{align}
    \left\|\frac{\partial y_{j}^{(q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}\right\|_F & \leq \left\|C_j^{(q)}\right\|_2 \left \|\frac{\partial h_j^{(q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}\right\|_F + \left\|h_j^{(q)}\right\|_F \left\|x_j^{(q)}\right\|_2 \nonumber \\ &\leq\left\|W_C^{(q)}\right\|_2 L_{x,q}J_{q} + H_{q} L_{x,q} = L_{x,q} \left(H_{q} + \left\|W_C^{(q)}\right\|_2J_{q}\right) \label{eq:multi_layer_own_output_jacobian_bound}.
\end{align}
This bound holds for every $q \in \{1, \dots, Q\}$ and $j \in \{1, \dots, S\}$ uniformly over all admissible input sequences. 

Substituting~\eqref{eq:multi_layer_own_output_jacobian_bound} into~\eqref{eq:output_jacobian_norm_bound}, we get:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:output_jacobian_norm_bound_with_single_layer}
    \left\|\frac{\partial y_t^{(Q)}}{\partial W^{(q)}}\right\|_F \leq L_{x,q} \left(H_{q} + \left\|W_C^{(q)}\right\|_2 J_{q}\right) \left(\sum_{j_{Q-1}=1}^{j_Q}\cdots\sum_{j_q=1}^{j_{q+1}}\left[ \prod_{q'=q+1}^{Q} \left\|\mathbf{K}_{j_{q'}, j_{q'-1}}^{(q')}\right\|_2\right] \right)
\end{equation}

Next, we derive a bound on the term:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:backprop_term_additon}
    \sum_{j_{Q-1}=1}^{j_Q}\cdots\sum_{j_q=1}^{j_{q+1}}\left[ \prod_{q'=q+1}^{Q} \left\|\mathbf{K}_{j_{q'}, j_{q'-1}}^{(q')}\right\|_2\right] 
\end{equation}
